Yeah… yes, I have. I mean, softball used to be the thing I’d look forward to all week, even when we lost—which we did a lot, honestly—and book club too, though sometimes I think we talked more about wine and school gossip than the actual book.

Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts going.

I’ll replay assessment deadlines, or something I said in class that I think came out wrong, or a mistake I made with a student’s work. And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.

And then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.

Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.

And then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”

And then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.

I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.

At first I told myself it was just stress, because everyone was stressed and the whole department was a mess, and I remember staying late with all these spreadsheets and rubrics… but yeah, that’s about when those thoughts started showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or your interest in activities over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I used to really look forward to things, like softball after school, and my book club—though honestly half the time book club was more about snacks and complaining about our weeks than the book, which was kind of the best part.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I used to spend whole weekends fussing over tomatoes and herbs, and the robotics club with the students used to be something I was proud of. Now I just… I either cancel, or I show up and kind of go through the motions. None of it really feels fun anymore. It’s like I know I used to enjoy these things, but I can’t quite get that feeling back.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. I mean, softball used to be the thing I’d look forward to all week, even when we lost—which we did a lot, honestly—and book club too, though sometimes I think we talked more about wine and school gossip than the actual book.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get weirdly excited about new basil coming in. Now I’ll look at it and think, “I should care about this,” but I just don’t really feel it. Same with robotics club at school. I still show up sometimes because the kids are counting on me, but it feels like I’m just… standing there, doing the steps. A lot of times I cancel things instead. None of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you've been moving around over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my energy is… pretty awful lately. I get through teaching, and then if there’s a department meeting after school, by the time I leave my body feels so heavy. Like even making dinner feels like this huge project.\n\nAnd it’s silly because dinner can be, you know, pasta or eggs or something, not a three-course meal. I used to be able to stay late and do emails, or answer a few parent messages, but now even opening the laptop feels like climbing a hill. I’ll sit there with my bag still by the door and think, “Okay, just get up,” and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts going.\n\nI’ll replay assessment deadlines, or something I said in class that I think came out wrong, or a mistake I made with a student’s work. And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.\n\nAnd then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.\n\nBut my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like my work trousers don’t sit right anymore, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days I just… don’t really eat until late afternoon. I’ll mean to grab something at lunch, and then the bell goes, or someone needs something, or I just look at the food and can’t be bothered. Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”\n\nAnd then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll sit there with my bag still by the door and think, “Okay, just get up,” and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts going.\n\nI’ll replay assessment deadlines, or something I said in class that I think came out wrong, or a mistake I made with a student’s work. And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.\n\nAnd then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.\n\nBut my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like my work trousers don’t sit right anymore, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days I just… don’t really eat until late afternoon. I’ll mean to grab something at lunch, and then the bell goes, or someone needs something, or I just look at the food and can’t be bothered. Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.

I’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.

Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”\n\nAnd then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.

There are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.

But significant improvement… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a proper return to normal in between, if that’s what you mean. It feels more like it’s just been… ongoing, with slightly less awful patches rather than a clear break.

I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more periods in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I’m sorry, I know I’m probably going in circles a bit.\n\nI can think of times I’ve been low or stressed, but not two separate periods like this. It’s more like this one has just… carried on, rather than there being lots of distinct episodes. I suppose that’s my best answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood and functioning between episodes of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.\n\nThere are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.\n\nBut significant improvement… I don’t know. No… I don’t think I have, not completely.

It’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, roughly. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load, which still annoys me because it was meant to be “temporary support,” and then somehow became my entire life for a while.

And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.\n\nAnd then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.\n\nBut my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like my work trousers don’t sit right anymore, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days I just… don’t really eat until late afternoon. I’ll mean to grab something at lunch, and then the bell goes, or someone needs something, or I just look at the food and can’t be bothered. Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”\n\nAnd then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more periods in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I’m sorry, I know I’m probably going in circles a bit.\n\nI can think of times I’ve been low or stressed, but not two separate periods like this. It’s more like this one has just… carried on, rather than there being lots of distinct episodes. I suppose that’s my best answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood and functioning between episodes of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.\n\nThere are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.\n\nBut significant improvement… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a proper return to normal in between, if that’s what you mean. It feels more like it’s just been… ongoing, with slightly less awful patches rather than a clear break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely back to your normal self for an extended period since these depressive thoughts started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have, not completely.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, roughly. I don’t think so, no. I know you’ve asked a couple of versions of this and I’m probably not being very neat about it, but I can’t really point to two separate “periods” with a proper improvement in between.

Like, sometimes I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then—this sounds awful, I know—but on the drive home from work, after school, when I’m going over everything in my head, I’ll wonder if my family and my students would be better off without me. I don’t want to do anything, I really don’t, but the fact that I’m even thinking that way… it frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: About… a year and a half, I think. Maybe a bit more—like a year and seven months. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching. At first I told myself it was just stress, because everyone was stressed and the whole department was a mess, and I remember staying late with all these spreadsheets and rubrics… but yeah, that’s about when those thoughts started showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or your interest in activities over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I used to really look forward to things, like softball after school, and my book club—though honestly half the time book club was more about snacks and complaining about our weeks than the book, which was kind of the best part.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I used to spend whole weekends fussing over tomatoes and herbs, and the robotics club with the students used to be something I was proud of. Now I just… I either cancel, or I show up and kind of go through the motions. None of it really feels fun anymore. It’s like I know I used to enjoy these things, but I can’t quite get that feeling back.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. I mean, softball used to be the thing I’d look forward to all week, even when we lost—which we did a lot, honestly—and book club too, though sometimes I think we talked more about wine and school gossip than the actual book.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get weirdly excited about new basil coming in. Now I’ll look at it and think, “I should care about this,” but I just don’t really feel it. Same with robotics club at school. I still show up sometimes because the kids are counting on me, but it feels like I’m just… standing there, doing the steps. A lot of times I cancel things instead. None of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you've been moving around over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my energy is… pretty awful lately. I get through teaching, and then if there’s a department meeting after school, by the time I leave my body feels so heavy. Like even making dinner feels like this huge project.\n\nAnd it’s silly because dinner can be, you know, pasta or eggs or something, not a three-course meal. I used to be able to stay late and do emails, or answer a few parent messages, but now even opening the laptop feels like climbing a hill. I’ll sit there with my bag still by the door and think, “Okay, just get up,” and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts going.\n\nI’ll replay assessment deadlines, or something I said in class that I think came out wrong, or a mistake I made with a student’s work. And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.\n\nAnd then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.\n\nBut my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like my work trousers don’t sit right anymore, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days I just… don’t really eat until late afternoon. I’ll mean to grab something at lunch, and then the bell goes, or someone needs something, or I just look at the food and can’t be bothered. Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”\n\nAnd then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more periods in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I’m sorry, I know I’m probably going in circles a bit.\n\nI can think of times I’ve been low or stressed, but not two separate periods like this. It’s more like this one has just… carried on, rather than there being lots of distinct episodes. I suppose that’s my best answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood and functioning between episodes of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.\n\nThere are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.\n\nBut significant improvement… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a proper return to normal in between, if that’s what you mean. It feels more like it’s just been… ongoing, with slightly less awful patches rather than a clear break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely back to your normal self for an extended period since these depressive thoughts started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have, not completely.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, roughly. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load, which still annoys me because it was meant to be “temporary support,” and then somehow became my entire life for a while.\n\nSince then, I’ve had better days, I suppose, or times where I can get through things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt properly back to myself for any extended stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more distinct periods in your life where you felt similarly depressed, with some period of improvement in between?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I know you’ve asked a couple of versions of this and I’m probably not being very neat about it, but I can’t really point to two separate “periods” with a proper improvement in between.\n\nIt’s more that I remember different bits of life feeling difficult, but not in a clearly divided way. And then I start second-guessing myself, because school years blur together so much—everything is term dates and marking cycles and whether Year 10 have remembered their folders, which they absolutely have not.\n\nBut, no… if you mean clearly separate times where I felt like this, then got better for a while, then it happened again—I haven’t noticed that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.1", "F33.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I know you’ve asked a couple of versions of this and I’m probably not being very neat about it, but I can’t really point to two separate “periods” with a proper improvement in between.\n\nIt’s more that I remember different bits of life feeling difficult, but not in a clearly divided way. And then I start second-guessing myself, because school years blur together so much—everything is term dates and marking cycles and whether Year 10 have remembered their folders, which they absolutely have not.\n\nBut, no… if you mean clearly separate times where I felt like this, then got better for a while, then it happened again—I haven’t noticed that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel depressed or have these thoughts?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 1 year and 7 months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. Not two months, anyway.

It’s been sort of continuous since I took on that STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load… about a year and seven months now, I think. There’ve been lighter patches, maybe, but not a proper couple of months where it all lifted. And it’s ridiculous because it was supposed to be just an extra bit of work, not something that swallowed everything, but—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I can’t think of a two-month stretch where I felt free of it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these thoughts that really scared me. Like, sometimes I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then—this sounds awful, I know—but on the drive home from work, after school, when I’m going over everything in my head, I’ll wonder if my family and my students would be better off without me. I don’t want to do anything, I really don’t, but the fact that I’m even thinking that way… it frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: About… a year and a half, I think. Maybe a bit more—like a year and seven months. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching. At first I told myself it was just stress, because everyone was stressed and the whole department was a mess, and I remember staying late with all these spreadsheets and rubrics… but yeah, that’s about when those thoughts started showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or your interest in activities over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I used to really look forward to things, like softball after school, and my book club—though honestly half the time book club was more about snacks and complaining about our weeks than the book, which was kind of the best part.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I used to spend whole weekends fussing over tomatoes and herbs, and the robotics club with the students used to be something I was proud of. Now I just… I either cancel, or I show up and kind of go through the motions. None of it really feels fun anymore. It’s like I know I used to enjoy these things, but I can’t quite get that feeling back.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. I mean, softball used to be the thing I’d look forward to all week, even when we lost—which we did a lot, honestly—and book club too, though sometimes I think we talked more about wine and school gossip than the actual book.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get weirdly excited about new basil coming in. Now I’ll look at it and think, “I should care about this,” but I just don’t really feel it. Same with robotics club at school. I still show up sometimes because the kids are counting on me, but it feels like I’m just… standing there, doing the steps. A lot of times I cancel things instead. None of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you've been moving around over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my energy is… pretty awful lately. I get through teaching, and then if there’s a department meeting after school, by the time I leave my body feels so heavy. Like even making dinner feels like this huge project.\n\nAnd it’s silly because dinner can be, you know, pasta or eggs or something, not a three-course meal. I used to be able to stay late and do emails, or answer a few parent messages, but now even opening the laptop feels like climbing a hill. I’ll sit there with my bag still by the door and think, “Okay, just get up,” and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts going.\n\nI’ll replay assessment deadlines, or something I said in class that I think came out wrong, or a mistake I made with a student’s work. And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.\n\nAnd then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.\n\nBut my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like my work trousers don’t sit right anymore, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days I just… don’t really eat until late afternoon. I’ll mean to grab something at lunch, and then the bell goes, or someone needs something, or I just look at the food and can’t be bothered. Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”\n\nAnd then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more periods in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I’m sorry, I know I’m probably going in circles a bit.\n\nI can think of times I’ve been low or stressed, but not two separate periods like this. It’s more like this one has just… carried on, rather than there being lots of distinct episodes. I suppose that’s my best answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood and functioning between episodes of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.\n\nThere are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.\n\nBut significant improvement… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a proper return to normal in between, if that’s what you mean. It feels more like it’s just been… ongoing, with slightly less awful patches rather than a clear break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely back to your normal self for an extended period since these depressive thoughts started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have, not completely.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, roughly. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder. I’m not sure. I mean, not in a way I would have called an episode at the time.

I’ve had stressful patches before — teaching sort of comes with those, doesn’t it? Especially around exam season, and there was one year where the timetable was just absurd, like someone had thrown darts at a spreadsheet. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as what you’re asking.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these thoughts that really scared me. Like, sometimes I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then—this sounds awful, I know—but on the drive home from work, after school, when I’m going over everything in my head, I’ll wonder if my family and my students would be better off without me. I don’t want to do anything, I really don’t, but the fact that I’m even thinking that way… it frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: About… a year and a half, I think. Maybe a bit more—like a year and seven months. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching. At first I told myself it was just stress, because everyone was stressed and the whole department was a mess, and I remember staying late with all these spreadsheets and rubrics… but yeah, that’s about when those thoughts started showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or your interest in activities over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I used to really look forward to things, like softball after school, and my book club—though honestly half the time book club was more about snacks and complaining about our weeks than the book, which was kind of the best part.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I used to spend whole weekends fussing over tomatoes and herbs, and the robotics club with the students used to be something I was proud of. Now I just… I either cancel, or I show up and kind of go through the motions. None of it really feels fun anymore. It’s like I know I used to enjoy these things, but I can’t quite get that feeling back.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. I mean, softball used to be the thing I’d look forward to all week, even when we lost—which we did a lot, honestly—and book club too, though sometimes I think we talked more about wine and school gossip than the actual book.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get weirdly excited about new basil coming in. Now I’ll look at it and think, “I should care about this,” but I just don’t really feel it. Same with robotics club at school. I still show up sometimes because the kids are counting on me, but it feels like I’m just… standing there, doing the steps. A lot of times I cancel things instead. None of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you've been moving around over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my energy is… pretty awful lately. I get through teaching, and then if there’s a department meeting after school, by the time I leave my body feels so heavy. Like even making dinner feels like this huge project.\n\nAnd it’s silly because dinner can be, you know, pasta or eggs or something, not a three-course meal. I used to be able to stay late and do emails, or answer a few parent messages, but now even opening the laptop feels like climbing a hill. I’ll sit there with my bag still by the door and think, “Okay, just get up,” and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts going.\n\nI’ll replay assessment deadlines, or something I said in class that I think came out wrong, or a mistake I made with a student’s work. And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.\n\nAnd then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.\n\nBut my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like my work trousers don’t sit right anymore, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days I just… don’t really eat until late afternoon. I’ll mean to grab something at lunch, and then the bell goes, or someone needs something, or I just look at the food and can’t be bothered. Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”\n\nAnd then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more periods in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I’m sorry, I know I’m probably going in circles a bit.\n\nI can think of times I’ve been low or stressed, but not two separate periods like this. It’s more like this one has just… carried on, rather than there being lots of distinct episodes. I suppose that’s my best answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood and functioning between episodes of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.\n\nThere are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.\n\nBut significant improvement… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a proper return to normal in between, if that’s what you mean. It feels more like it’s just been… ongoing, with slightly less awful patches rather than a clear break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely back to your normal self for an extended period since these depressive thoughts started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have, not completely.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, roughly. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load, which still annoys me because it was meant to be “temporary support,” and then somehow became my entire life for a while.\n\nSince then, I’ve had better days, I suppose, or times where I can get through things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt properly back to myself for any extended stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more distinct periods in your life where you felt similarly depressed, with some period of improvement in between?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more periods in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I’m sorry, I know I’m probably going in circles a bit.\n\nI can think of times I’ve been low or stressed, but not two separate periods like this. It’s more like this one has just… carried on, rather than there being lots of distinct episodes. I suppose that’s my best answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood and functioning between episodes of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.\n\nThere are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.\n\nBut significant improvement… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a proper return to normal in between, if that’s what you mean. It feels more like it’s just been… ongoing, with slightly less awful patches rather than a clear break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely back to your normal self for an extended period since these depressive thoughts started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have, not completely.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, roughly. And then I start second-guessing myself, because school years blur together so much—everything is term dates and marking cycles and whether Year 10 have remembered their folders, which they absolutely have not.\n\nBut, no… if you mean clearly separate times where I felt like this, then got better for a while, then it happened again—I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel depressed or have these thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these thoughts that really scared me. Like, sometimes I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then—this sounds awful, I know—but on the drive home from work, after school, when I’m going over everything in my head, I’ll wonder if my family and my students would be better off without me. I don’t want to do anything, I really don’t, but the fact that I’m even thinking that way… it frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: About… a year and a half, I think. Maybe a bit more—like a year and seven months. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching. At first I told myself it was just stress, because everyone was stressed and the whole department was a mess, and I remember staying late with all these spreadsheets and rubrics… but yeah, that’s about when those thoughts started showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or your interest in activities over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I used to really look forward to things, like softball after school, and my book club—though honestly half the time book club was more about snacks and complaining about our weeks than the book, which was kind of the best part.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I used to spend whole weekends fussing over tomatoes and herbs, and the robotics club with the students used to be something I was proud of. Now I just… I either cancel, or I show up and kind of go through the motions. None of it really feels fun anymore. It’s like I know I used to enjoy these things, but I can’t quite get that feeling back.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. I mean, softball used to be the thing I’d look forward to all week, even when we lost—which we did a lot, honestly—and book club too, though sometimes I think we talked more about wine and school gossip than the actual book.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get weirdly excited about new basil coming in. Now I’ll look at it and think, “I should care about this,” but I just don’t really feel it. Same with robotics club at school. I still show up sometimes because the kids are counting on me, but it feels like I’m just… standing there, doing the steps. A lot of times I cancel things instead. None of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you've been moving around over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my energy is… pretty awful lately. I get through teaching, and then if there’s a department meeting after school, by the time I leave my body feels so heavy. Like even making dinner feels like this huge project.\n\nAnd it’s silly because dinner can be, you know, pasta or eggs or something, not a three-course meal. I used to be able to stay late and do emails, or answer a few parent messages, but now even opening the laptop feels like climbing a hill. I’ll sit there with my bag still by the door and think, “Okay, just get up,” and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great. I can be completely exhausted, like barely keeping my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts going.\n\nI’ll replay assessment deadlines, or something I said in class that I think came out wrong, or a mistake I made with a student’s work. And then I start thinking about the marking pile, and whether I forgot to email someone back—honestly it’s ridiculous, because none of it is getting solved at midnight.\n\nAnd then a lot of mornings I wake up around 4 a.m., and that’s kind of it. I might doze a little, but I never really get back to proper sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, because the scale is in the cupboard with all the bathroom stuff I keep meaning to organize—there’s probably sunscreen in there from, like, three summers ago.\n\nBut my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like my work trousers don’t sit right anymore, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days I just… don’t really eat until late afternoon. I’ll mean to grab something at lunch, and then the bell goes, or someone needs something, or I just look at the food and can’t be bothered. Then suddenly it’s nearly the end of the day and I realize I haven’t had anything proper.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt that way about before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I have. It’s like, if a lesson goes a bit flat or one of the kids looks confused, I don’t just think, “Okay, that explanation didn’t land.” I take it as this big sign that I’m useless, that I’m letting all of them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll keep going over it later, like while I’m washing up or packing my bag for the next day—well, half-packing it, because I always forget the board pens—and I’ll replay the student’s face or something I said, and it turns into, “I’m a terrible teacher.”\n\nAnd then it kind of spreads, if that makes sense. I start thinking I’m probably a terrible mother too, like I’m failing at everything, even when it’s something small. I didn’t used to be quite that harsh with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything quite like this before. Not for this long, anyway.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. Which at the time I kept telling myself was just “a busy term,” you know, everyone’s busy, the photocopier’s always jammed, there’s always some new spreadsheet someone wants filled in…\n\nBut it didn’t really lift after that. It just sort of kept going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes like this before in your life, where you felt similarly depressed for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure. I mean, I’ve had stressful patches before, definitely—university deadlines, first year teaching, that sort of thing, and I remember crying in the stock cupboard once because Year 9 had been absolutely feral and the printer had eaten all my worksheets, which sounds silly now.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d call any of those the same as this, or as extended. They usually had a reason and then passed, if that makes sense. This feels… harder to compare. So I suppose, no, not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than one in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had times where I was low or stressed, like everyone has, and I can remember getting overwhelmed with work before—teaching does that, especially when you’ve got a class right after lunch and they’ve all had fizzy drinks, or there’s some new marking policy that changes three times in a week.\n\nBut this feels different from those. So… no, I can’t really point to another proper period like this before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more periods in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I’m sorry, I know I’m probably going in circles a bit.\n\nI can think of times I’ve been low or stressed, but not two separate periods like this. It’s more like this one has just… carried on, rather than there being lots of distinct episodes. I suppose that’s my best answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant improvement in your mood and functioning between episodes of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “between episodes,” really, because I don’t think I’ve had separate episodes like that.\n\nThere are moments where I function a bit better, I suppose—like if I manage to get through a school day without everything feeling completely impossible, or if a lesson goes surprisingly well, which can happen with the right class, especially if they’re doing something practical and not just staring at me like I’ve asked them to translate ancient Greek.\n\nBut significant improvement… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a proper return to normal in between, if that’s what you mean. It feels more like it’s just been… ongoing, with slightly less awful patches rather than a clear break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely back to your normal self for an extended period since these depressive thoughts started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have, not completely.\n\nIt’s been going on for about a year and seven months now, roughly. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load, which still annoys me because it was meant to be “temporary support,” and then somehow became my entire life for a while.\n\nSince then, I’ve had better days, I suppose, or times where I can get through things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt properly back to myself for any extended stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more distinct periods in your life where you felt similarly depressed, with some period of improvement in between?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I know you’ve asked a couple of versions of this and I’m probably not being very neat about it, but I can’t really point to two separate “periods” with a proper improvement in between.\n\nIt’s more that I remember different bits of life feeling difficult, but not in a clearly divided way. And then I start second-guessing myself, because school years blur together so much—everything is term dates and marking cycles and whether Year 10 have remembered their folders, which they absolutely have not.\n\nBut, no… if you mean clearly separate times where I felt like this, then got better for a while, then it happened again—I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you didn't feel depressed or have these thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not two months, anyway.\n\nIt’s been sort of continuous since I took on that STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load… about a year and seven months now, I think. There’ve been lighter patches, maybe, but not a proper couple of months where it all lifted. And it’s ridiculous because it was supposed to be just an extra bit of work, not something that swallowed everything, but—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I can’t think of a two-month stretch where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any depressive episodes before the one that started about a year and seven months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, not in a way I would have called an episode at the time.\n\nI’ve had stressful patches before — teaching sort of comes with those, doesn’t it? Especially around exam season, and there was one year where the timetable was just absurd, like someone had thrown darts at a spreadsheet. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as what you’re asking.\n\nBefore this last year and a bit, I can’t really point to a clear period where I thought, yes, this is the same thing happening. So… I suppose I’d say I’m not aware of any definite ones before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt completely back to your normal self at any point since these depressive thoughts started, even if only for a short while?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there have been odd moments where I’ve been distracted enough to feel a bit more like myself — usually when I’m in the middle of something practical at school, or when someone needs something immediately, which is most of teaching, honestly.\n\nBut completely back to normal? I don’t think so. Since I took on that STEM assessment redesign alongside everything else — about a year and seven months ago now — it’s felt like this has just been… there. Sometimes quieter, maybe, but not gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely better for at least two months before these depressive thoughts started again?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there was a break like that. It’s all sort of tied to when I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load, and that was… about a year and seven months ago now.\n\nI keep thinking maybe if I’d organized it differently, or if the department had actually agreed on the marking criteria sooner — sorry, that’s not really the point. But no, I can’t think of a two-month stretch where I felt completely better and then it started again. It’s been more continuous than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.1", "F33.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to look forward to softball, book club, my garden, and robotics club, but now I mostly go through the motions or cancel because none of it feels enjoyable anymore.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Most school days I forget or avoid eating until late afternoon, and over the past year my clothes have gotten noticeably looser even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: I can be exhausted and still lie awake replaying assessment deadlines and classroom mistakes, then wake up around 4 a.m. and never really get back to sleep.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: By the time I get through teaching and a department meeting, my body feels so heavy that even making dinner or answering a few parent emails feels like climbing a hill.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: When a lesson goes badly or a student looks confused, I take it as proof that I’m letting everyone down and I keep replaying it like I’m a terrible teacher and mother.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’ll read the same student response three or four times and still not know what score to give it, and simple choices like which lab supplies to order can stall me for an hour.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I’ve started catching myself thinking it would be easier if I didn’t wake up, and sometimes on the drive home I wonder whether my family and students would be better off without me, which scares me because I don’t want to act on it.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m falling behind on grading and lesson planning in ways that affect my classroom, and the grading mistake scared me because I used to be meticulous. I don’t want to do anything, I really don’t, but the fact that I’m even thinking that way… it frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: About… a year and a half, I think. Maybe a bit more—like a year and seven months. It started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching. At first I told myself it was just stress, because everyone was stressed and the whole department was a mess, and I remember staying late with all these spreadsheets and rubrics… but yeah, that’s about when those thoughts started showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or your interest in activities over the past year and a half?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I used to really look forward to things, like softball after school, and my book club—though honestly half the time book club was more about snacks and complaining about our weeks than the book, which was kind of the best part.\n\nAnd my garden, too. Now I just… I either cancel, or I show up and kind of go through the motions. None of it really feels fun anymore. It’s like I know I used to enjoy these things, but I can’t quite get that feeling back.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have.
